  - **Files:** `src/wake_word.py` (`monitor_for_wake_word` + the `_barge_model` singleton + `_get_barge_model`); `src/text_to_speech.py` (`speak_streaming` gains `interrupt_event` — non-blocking play + 20 ms poll loop, owner-thread `sd.stop()`; a `stop_playback` helper was added then removed once the crash fix moved the cut onto the owner thread); `src/llm.py` (`stream_response` gains `interrupt_event` + the `final.container` capture for Issue B); `main.py` (`_barge_in_enabled`/`_BARGE_IN_ENABLED`; `process_question` gains a `session` param, spawns the monitor, returns `interrupted: bool`; `listen_loop` `barged` flag — skip drain after a barge-in; `speak`→`text_only` rename; `_is_dismissal` trailing-courtesy strip + `_TRAILING_COURTESY`); `scripts/barge_stutter_soak.py` (the stutter-soak instrument); `.env.example` (`JARVIS_BARGE_IN`); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons:** (1) **Audio APIs are thread-affine — the owning thread does ALL the calls.** WASAPI/COM streams are STA-bound (thread-affine); `sd.stop()` from a non-owner thread is a native crash, not a soft failure. The project already had this documented for `sd.play()`; the blueprint should have been checked against it and wasn't. When a design has thread A signal thread B, the signal is a plain `threading.Event` — never a callback that reaches into B's resources. (2) **A callback parameter hides which thread the work runs on.** The `on_detect` callback made it *look* like the monitor "just calls a function"; it disguised a cross-STA audio call. Deleting the parameter (not just leaving it unwired) made the safe design *structural*. (3) **`drain()` is correct after a pause, wrong after an interruption.** The same buffer-clear is right for a normal wake word (the speaker pauses) and wrong for a barge-in (the speaker is mid-sentence). A "clear stale audio" step must know *why* the audio is there. (4) **A new feature surfaces latent bugs in the paths it newly exercises.** The `speak`-shadowing bug sat dormant ~7 milestones because nothing hit the apology path; M52's testing did. Treat "an old path just ran for the first time in a while" as a place defects hide.

- [x] **Milestone 53 — reminders & timers**:
  - **What ships:** Jarvis's first "act at a future time" capability. Everything before this was present-tense — a question in, an answer out. Now "remind me in 8 minutes to take the laundry out" or "set a timer for 2 minutes" schedules a spoken reminder that fires later, on its own, with no further prompting — the single most-missed everyday-assistant primitive, and it had been entirely absent.
  - **Three custom tools, not a deterministic intent matcher.** `set_reminder` / `list_reminders` / `cancel_reminder` are M13-pattern tools. The natural-language *time* parsing — "in an hour and a half", "at 6:30", "tomorrow morning" — is exactly Claude's strength; a regex matcher would be brittle. This is the deliberate opposite of M51's `_is_dismissal` (deterministic *because* it gates loop control and must be certain): reminder-setting is a capability, so the model parses. `set_reminder` takes `delay_seconds` XOR `at` (ISO 8601) — relative requests need no clock knowledge (the tool computes from `now()`); absolute ones carry a datetime Claude builds from the date it already has. The system prompt deliberately does NOT carry time-of-day — that would bust the prompt cache every minute.
  - **The file is the IPC.** `set_reminder` appends to `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Jarvis\reminders.json`; the scheduler thread re-reads it each poll. Tool and scheduler are fully decoupled — no runtime object threaded through `stream_response` (the tools fit the plain `_ClientTool` `execute(dict) -> str` shape). One module lock in `src/reminders.py` guards every read/write; the write is atomic (temp file + `os.replace`) so a crash mid-write can never corrupt the store. The JSON IS the source of truth and survives a restart.
  - **A poll-loop scheduler thread**, M34-watcher style — spawned in `main()` alongside the Announcer, wakes every ~10 s, fires anything due via `_announce`. A poll loop (not N `threading.Timer`s) trivially handles app restart: it polls immediately on start, so reminders that came due while Jarvis was off fire on the first poll — flagged "this was due while I was offline" when >90 s overdue. Reminders fire through `_announce` — a reminder is a proactive announce, so it rides the same WASAPI-safe Announcer path as a security alert (the thread-affinity rule from the M52 post-mortem, applied correctly by reuse this time).
  - **Small companion polish:** `_announce` gained a `label` param (the console emoji tag) — the queue tuple went `(text, on_done)` → `(text, on_done, label)`, contained entirely to `main.py`. A reminder shows as ⏰ in the console, not the security 🚨. (The 🚨-for-everything was a pre-existing minor wart — knowledge-reindex and face-enroll announces wore it too; the `label` param fixes the class, defaulting to 🚨 so existing callers are unchanged.)
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** one-shot reminders only — **recurring** ("every day at 7am") is the clean follow-on, and the hook a scheduled "good morning" briefing would build on. Speaks + console only (no Discord/email push on fire — that's a small follow-on). No snooze; edit = cancel + re-set. Available to phone-origin turns (NOT in `_RESTRICTED_DENY`): a reminder just speaks on the PC and touches nothing destructive, so there was no reason to block it.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; full-dependency import smoke PASS. **16-case headless functional test** PASS — store CRUD, all three executors, every rejection path (past time, delay+at both, neither, empty message, negative delay), `pop_due` atomicity + destructiveness, the belated-vs-normal fire text. **Scheduler-thread test** PASS — the real `run_scheduler` thread polled, fired a due reminder through `announce()`, left a future one untouched, exited cleanly on the stop event. **Live-validated 2026-05-21:** all four intents routed correctly (`reminder_set` ×2, `reminder_list`, `reminder_cancel` — every one `=yes` in telemetry), natural confirmations, the list read both pending items back with times, cancel-by-query worked and Claude noted the surviving reminder. **The decisive result — persistence across a restart:** a reminder set in one session survived a clean shutdown + relaunch and fired in the *next* process (`[reminders] firing r593d25` → `[announce] Sir, a reminder — …`). The cancelled timer correctly never fired. A clean ship — no bugs surfaced.
  - **Files:** `src/reminders.py` (NEW — the JSON store with atomic write + module lock, the three tool schemas + executors, the `run_scheduler` poll loop, `_fire_text` with the belated flag); `src/llm.py` (the three tools imported + registered in `_CLIENT_TOOLS` + added to the `tools` list; a "Reminders & timers" system-prompt section, tools 18–20); `main.py` (`run_scheduler` thread spawned by `main()`, `reminder_stop` for clean shutdown; `_announce` gained the `label` param and the announce-queue tuple grew to 3); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons:** (1) **The file can be the IPC.** Decoupling the tool (writer) from the scheduler (reader) through `reminders.json` itself — instead of threading a runtime object through `stream_response` — kept the tools as pure `_ClientTool` functions and made the design restart-safe for free (the store *is* the persistence). When two components need to share state and one of them must survive a restart anyway, the persistent file is often the cleanest channel between them. (2) **A poll loop beats N timers when restart is in the picture.** One thread that re-reads state each tick handles app restart, overdue-on-startup, and clock changes with no extra code; N `threading.Timer`s handle none of them. (3) **Match the parser to the job.** M51 made `_is_dismissal` deterministic *because* it gates loop control; M53 makes time-parsing Claude's job *because* it's fuzzy natural language. "Use the model for judgement, not for plumbing" cuts both ways — the test is whether the decision must be certain (deterministic) or interpretive (the model). (4) **A capability built once can be foundation.** The scheduler isn't just reminders — it's the machinery proactive homelab monitoring and a scheduled "good morning" briefing will reuse. Building the bounded thing well opened three wish-list items, not one.

- [x] **Milestone 54 — recurring reminders**:
  - **What ships:** the M53 follow-on — a reminder can now repeat on a schedule. *"Remind me every weekday at 9 to stand up"*, *"every 30 minutes to check the print"*, *"on the 1st of every month to pay rent."* When a recurring reminder fires it isn't consumed — it re-arms for its next occurrence.
  - **Three recurrence kinds, no full calendar engine.** `set_reminder` gained an optional nested `repeat` param; omit it and the M53 one-shot behaviour is byte-identical. The three kinds: `interval` (a fixed period in seconds — "every 30 minutes"), `weekly` (a weekday set + a time-of-day — "every weekday at 9", "every Monday at 6pm", and "every day at 7am" as all seven days), `monthly` (a day-of-month + a time, clamped to the month's last day via `calendar.monthrange`, so day 31 means "the last day of every month"). Between them they cover the realistic 90%; monthly was pulled into v1 rather than deferred — ~15 LOC closed a real gap (monthly bills have no workaround in interval/weekly). Deferred: yearly, "every other Monday", nth-weekday — genuinely rare for a voice assistant.
  - **Claude parses, the tool computes.** `set_reminder` takes the structured `repeat` spec (Claude maps "every weekday at 9" → `{kind:weekly, weekdays:[0,1,2,3,4], time:"09:00"}`); `_validate_repeat` normalises + rejects malformed specs with a voice-friendly error; `_next_occurrence(repeat, after)` does the date math for all three kinds. For a recurring reminder the first occurrence is *derived* from the recurrence (no `delay_seconds`/`at` needed). weekly/monthly recompute the wall-clock time each occurrence, so they're DST-correct; interval is for sub-day periods where that's moot.
  - **Re-arm, not re-create — and catch-up, not stampede.** `pop_due` became recurrence-aware: a one-shot is removed on fire; a recurring reminder is re-armed *in place* — **same id** (so `list`/`cancel` stay stable across occurrences), `fire_at` advanced. The next slot is computed from `now()`, not the stale `fire_at` — so however long Jarvis was off, a missed recurring reminder fires exactly **once** on restart, then resumes. There is structurally only ever one record per recurring reminder, so a backlog stampede is impossible by construction.
  - **Companion fix — `cancel_reminder` reports what remains.** The live test surfaced it: after cancelling the stretch reminder Claude said "the trash reminder remains" — but that one had already fired. Not a tool bug (cancel worked) — Claude *inferred* "what's left" from a stale earlier list in the conversation. Fix: `cancel_reminder` now appends the actually-still-pending reminders to its result, so Claude states what remains from fact, not memory.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; import smoke PASS. **29-case headless functional test** PASS — every `_validate_repeat` path (all three kinds + each rejection), `_next_occurrence` for all three including the monthly clamp (day 31 → April 30, → Feb 28) and weekly's already-passed-today handling, `_describe_repeat`, set + list with recurrence, and the critical `pop_due` re-arm (recurring reminder kept its id with a future `fire_at`, one-shot removed). Plus a targeted test of the cancel-reports-remaining fix. **Live-validated 2026-05-21:** *"remind me every two minutes to stretch"* → routed to `set_reminder` with the right `repeat` spec, confirmed "every two minutes"; `list_reminders` read it back as "stretch every two minutes"; and the decisive result — **the reminder fired twice with the same id `r81c539`** (`[reminders] firing r81c539: stretch` … `[reminders] firing r81c539: stretch`), the re-arm working in production; *"cancel my stretch reminder"* stopped it (no further fires). A one-shot from a prior session fired once and was gone. Clean ship.
  - **Files:** `src/reminders.py` (`_parse_hhmm`, `_human_time`, `_validate_repeat`, `_next_occurrence`, `_describe_repeat`; `add` takes a `repeat` kwarg; `pop_due` re-arms recurring reminders; `execute_set_reminder` handles `repeat`; `execute_list_reminders` describes recurrence; `execute_cancel_reminder` reports what remains; `SET_REMINDER_TOOL` schema gains the nested `repeat` object); `src/llm.py` (the system-prompt tool entry updated for recurrence); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry. `main.py` untouched — the scheduler already calls `pop_due`, now recurrence-aware internally.

  **Reusable lessons:** (1) **A small fixed taxonomy beats a general engine.** Three recurrence kinds — interval / weekly / monthly — cover the realistic 90% of "remind me regularly" in ~120 lines; a full RFC-5545 / cron engine would be far more code for cases (nth-weekday, "every other week") a voice assistant rarely hears. Build the taxonomy people actually speak. (2) **Re-arm in place to keep identity stable.** A recurring thing that's deleted-and-recreated each cycle gets a new id every time, breaking "cancel the X reminder" between fires. Advancing the existing record (same id, new time) keeps every handle the caller and the LLM hold valid across occurrences. (3) **Compute the next slot from `now`, not the last scheduled time.** That single choice is what turns "Jarvis was off for a day" from a stampede of missed fires into one clean catch-up — the difference between a backlog and a resume. (4) **A tool should return the facts the model will be tempted to guess.** Claude narrated "the trash reminder remains" from a stale earlier turn because the cancel result didn't say. Returning the current still-pending set with the result removed the temptation — when a tool's caller will editorialise about adjacent state, hand it that state.

- [x] **Milestone 55 — the "good morning" briefing**:
  - **What ships:** "Jarvis, good morning" → one composed spoken reply — today's weather, sports + gaming headlines, anything the security watcher caught overnight, and the reminders due today. The films' JARVIS handing Tony the state of his world before coffee. The single most "it's really Jarvis" moment in the project so far, and it works through the phone.
  - **One composition tool — `get_briefing` (`src/briefing.py`).** Rather than hoping Claude separately calls weather, then news, then..., `get_briefing` gathers every section itself and returns one block; Claude calls it once and voices it. That makes "the briefing" a *defined, reliable* thing, not an emergent behaviour. It calls `execute_weather_tool` / `execute_news_tool` directly, reads the M35 security-events dir, and reads the M53/M54 reminder store — each section wrapped so a dead feed or a weather outage degrades *that one line*, never the whole briefing (proven live — see below). Param-less; reads its own `JARVIS_HOME_LOCATION` / `JARVIS_HOME_UNITS` env (the wolfram/tmdb self-contained pattern, not the core Config). Available to phone-origin turns (read-only, harmless).
  - **Sports + gaming, not general news — a deliberate product call.** General/world news is draining; a *morning* briefing should set the day up, not weigh it down. So the news section is sports (NHL/NFL/WWE) and gaming (PlayStation/Nintendo) — two new categories added to `get_news`'s `_FEEDS` registry (the M47 design explicitly invites this — "categories are the only thing the schema is coupled to"). Side-benefit: `get_news` now covers sports/gaming generally, retiring the M47 wart where such a query escalated to web_search. **Every feed URL was verified live** through `http_util`'s fetch path before being committed (the M47 discipline) — two WWE candidates were dropped for 301/308 redirects, the M47 redirect landmine confirmed yet again.
  - **Companion fix 1 — the M48 phone state-pill (built, never worked).** The PWA's 4-state pill was already in M48.1's snapshot/state plumbing — the server broadcasts `{type:"state"}`, the PWA has a `STATE_COLOR` map and handles the message. But it never reflected anything: `State` (in `tray.py`) is an enum whose `.value` is an **(r,g,b) tuple** for the tray icon, and `ui.py` shipped *that tuple* to the phone, so the PWA received `state:[255,204,0]`, missed `STATE_COLOR`, and rendered garbage. Fixed with a one-liner — `ui.py` now sends `state.name.lower()`, which matches the PWA's keys. CLAUDE.md had listed the state-pill as "the one remaining M48 item, NEXT"; a code read said "already done" — the real signal was an actual device report that the pill wasn't visible on the phone. The M48 thin-client arc is now genuinely complete.
  - **Companion fix 2 — the briefing must always re-run.** First live test: a second "good morning" in the same session returned *"I already gave you the full briefing"* — Claude saw the earlier briefing in conversation history and declined to re-run the tool. A briefing is a *fresh-state* request, like asking the time. Fixed in the `get_briefing` prompt entry: ALWAYS re-run, every time, never answer from an earlier briefing in memory. Live-validated — 3 consecutive "good morning"s → 3 fresh briefings.
  - **Companion fix 3 — `weather.py` geocode tolerates a "City, State" suffix.** The briefing's weather kept failing while a standalone `get_weather` succeeded — and `weather_tool=yes` on every briefing showed Claude silently compensating. A measured diagnostic (not a guess) was decisive: `_geocode("<City>, <ST>")` misses 3/3, `_geocode("<City>")` resolves 3/3 — Open-Meteo's geocoder matches a bare place NAME and rejects a comma-suffix, which is exactly what anyone naturally writes in `JARVIS_HOME_LOCATION`. Fixed in `weather.py`: `_geocode` retries with the pre-comma part when the full string misses — benefits every caller of `get_weather`, not just the briefing. (The `.env.example` comment had even offered `'London, UK'` as an example — it would have failed identically; with the fix it now genuinely works.)
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** on-demand only. The *scheduled* briefing ("brief me every morning at 7" — the M54 recurring-reminder hook) is the clean follow-on: a recurring reminder currently speaks a static string, so wiring one to *trigger the composition* is a bit more plumbing. Deferred, noted.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; import smoke PASS. `get_news` sports/gaming categories live-tested (real ESPN / WrestlingInc / Push Square / Nintendo Life headlines). Full briefing composed live (5/5 sections). The state-pill fix verified by name-match (`ui.py` now ships exactly the PWA's `STATE_COLOR` keys); the geocode fix verified deterministically (a "City, ST" string now resolves; the briefing weather section populates). **Live-validated 2026-05-22** on PC and phone: "good morning" → routed to `get_briefing`, natural composed briefing; 3 consecutive briefings (the re-run fix); turn 5 of an earlier run *proved the fail-soft contract* — a transient weather-geocode hiccup degraded that one line and the briefing still delivered sports/gaming/security.
  - **Files:** `src/briefing.py` (NEW — the `get_briefing` tool: weather/news/security/reminders composition, each section fail-soft); `src/news.py` (`_FEEDS` + `_CATEGORY_ALIASES` + the schema enum + label map gain `sports` and `gaming`); `src/weather.py` (`_geocode` split into `_geocode_query` + a comma-suffix-tolerant `_geocode`); `src/ui.py` (`set_state` ships `state.name.lower()`, not `State.value`); `src/llm.py` (`get_briefing` registered + the system-prompt entry #21 with the always-re-run rule; the `get_news` entry #15 gains sports/gaming); `.env.example` (`JARVIS_HOME_LOCATION` / `JARVIS_HOME_UNITS`); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons:** (1) **Verify the summary against the code — but the running system is the final oracle.** A code read concluded the state-pill was "done" (the plumbing was all there); only an actual device check exposed that it never *worked*. Documentation can drift; code can be present-but-broken; the deployed behaviour is the truth. (2) **Measure the failure, don't theorise it.** The briefing weather looked "intermittent" — it wasn't; a 3×-per-variant `_geocode` probe proved it was a consistent "City, State"-suffix rejection. One diagnostic script settled what a paragraph of speculation couldn't (the M44.3 instinct, in miniature). (3) **A composition tool should own its composition.** Letting Claude *decide* to call weather + news + ... separately is unreliable; one `get_briefing` that gathers everything makes the feature deterministic — and fail-soft per section means it degrades a line, never the whole thing. (4) **"Fresh-state" requests must re-run, every time.** A briefing, the weather, the time — asking again means *give me the current answer*, never "I already told you." When a tool answers a fresh-state question, its prompt entry must explicitly forbid answering from an earlier call in the conversation.

- [x] **Milestone 56 — proactive homelab monitoring**:
  - **What ships:** Jarvis's first *unprompted heartbeat*. A background thread watches the homelab and speaks up ON ITS OWN when something breaks — the Plex laptop goes unreachable, Plex Media Server stops responding, or a disk runs low — and again when it recovers (*"Plex is responding again, sir."*). Plus an on-demand `homelab_status` tool ("Jarvis, how's the homelab?"). Every milestone before this was *reactive* — you speak, he answers; even the M55 briefing only fires when asked. M56 is the reactive → proactive jump: Jarvis now notices things and reports them. The films' JARVIS flagging a problem before Tony asks.
  - **Architecture — almost entirely recombination.** New module `src/homelab_monitor.py`, a near-copy of patterns already trusted: thread model = `reminders.run_scheduler` (one defensive daemon poll loop, a stop `Event`, every poll wrapped so a bad check can't kill the thread); lifecycle/opt-in = `SecurityWatcher` (`activate()`/`deactivate()`/`is_active()`, a tray toggle, an `.env` flag, default OFF); remote reach = the existing `PlexLaptopClient` — its `run()` is already lock-serialised, so the monitor thread *shares the one SSH connection* the tool path uses, no new auth surface; voice out = `main.py`'s `_announce` (the WASAPI-safe Announcer path), tagged 🖥 to read distinctly from 🚨/⏰; phone push = a new `notifications.send_discord_message` (the generic, no-attachment counterpart to the deterrent-hardwired `send_discord_alert`).
  - **The interesting part — the alert state machine.** A monitoring tool lives or dies on *alert hygiene*: the naive version polls every minute and, while Plex is down, says "Plex is down" *every minute* — alert fatigue, the thing that makes people mute their monitoring. The fix is three classic ideas, each a handful of lines, all in `_CheckTracker` (deliberately isolated from all I/O so it is trivially unit-testable):
    1. **Flap damping / hysteresis.** A single failed poll is noise (a dropped packet, a GC pause). A check must fail `JARVIS_HOMELAB_FAIL_THRESHOLD` polls IN A ROW (default 3) before OK → DOWN. This is Nagios's soft-vs-hard state (`max_check_attempts`); it is a Schmitt trigger — don't let a sensor chatter on the boundary.
    2. **Edge-triggered, not level-triggered.** The alert fires ONCE, on the transition; stay-down polls are silent. Recovery (DOWN → OK) is its own edge and fires one "it's back" — closing the loop matters.
    3. **Dependency suppression.** `plex` and `disk` declare `depends_on="host"`. If the laptop itself is unreachable, those two checks are skipped (and their trackers reset) rather than each firing — you get ONE "laptop is down", not three. Nagios parent/child host dependencies: don't page for fifty services when the router they sit behind is what died.
  - **What it watches (v1):** host reachability (TCP connect to :22 — no ICMP, no admin, no subprocess), Plex liveness (`GET /identity` — 200 + XML, no auth token), and disk space on the laptop (worst fixed drive < `JARVIS_HOMELAB_DISK_MIN_PCT` free, default 10%). Built on an extensible check registry — a check is one function. The homelab has exactly one monitored host, so v1 is MEDIA-HOST-only by design, not by omission.
  - **A clean seam, not a parsed display string.** The disk check needed structured numbers; `execute_plex_laptop_health(..., "disk")` returns a voice-*formatted* string. Rather than regex a display string back apart, added a public `plex_laptop.fetch_disk_usage(client)` → `[{id,total,free,pct_free}]` that reuses the exact same `_HEALTH_DISK_PS` script — the right seam, ~30 LOC, robust to nothing.
  - **`homelab_status` tool (the free "status report" down-payment).** Param-less custom tool; probes everything fresh and returns a short per-check roll-up. A *fresh-state* request like the briefing — the prompt entry says run it every time, never answer from an earlier check. NOT in `_RESTRICTED_DENY` (read-only, touches nothing — the phone may ask). Reaches the live monitor via a module-global set in `HomelabMonitor.__init__` — the same tool/runtime decoupling `reminders.py` gets from its JSON file, via an object here since this state is in-memory, not persisted.
  - **Demo-safe by construction.** Default OFF; opt-in via `JARVIS_HOMELAB_MONITOR` or the tray's "Homelab monitoring" toggle (the Security-mode pattern, exactly). Doesn't touch the listen loop. Nothing else in the system depends on it and nothing can be destabilised by it — least-privilege, opt-in ("Jarvis stays Jarvis").
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS (full close-out gate). `_CheckTracker` unit test — `scripts/homelab_alert_test.py`, an asserting harness in the `leak_repro.py`/`barge_stutter_soak.py` instrument tradition — **16/16 PASS**: clean stream silent, 2-fail blip damped, 'down' on exactly the 3rd, stay-down silent, one 'recovered', no phantom recovery, re-down after recovery, counter resets on any OK (consecutive not cumulative), `reset()`, threshold-1. Import + wiring smoke PASS (`homelab_status` registered in `_CLIENT_TOOLS`, correctly absent from `_RESTRICTED_DENY`). **Live-tested against the real MEDIA-HOST: all three checks ran true** — host reachable (TCP :22), Plex responding (`/identity` 2xx), disk healthy (worst drive 27% free); `status_report()` and `execute_homelab_status({})` returned identically.
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** on-demand status + background monitor. Not built: a stay-down *re-reminder* cadence (one alert per edge for now); CPU/RAM sustained-high checks (noisier — disk/up-down is the high-signal set); email push (Discord is the instant-phone channel; email is M38's security path). A console "🖥 monitoring" indicator was deliberately skipped — the tray checkmark is the indicator, less UI surface = lower risk.

  - **Live-test fix (same session, 2026-05-22) — dependency suppression must FREEZE the tracker, not reset it.** The first live test ran the exact scenario that exposed it: stop Plex (→ "Plex stopped responding"), then take the whole host down (→ "MEDIA-HOST unreachable"), then bring the host back (→ "MEDIA-HOST back online"). The missing line: there was never a "Plex is responding again". Cause: while the host was DOWN, `_poll_once` skipped the dependency-suppressed `plex` check AND called `tracker.reset()` on it every poll — erasing the fact that Plex was DOWN. So when the host recovered and Plex was up, the tracker saw OK→OK (no edge) instead of DOWN→OK, and stayed silent. The host outage *swallowed* the child recovery — an alert that opened and never closed (and, symmetrically, a still-down child would have re-fired a duplicate DOWN). Fix: in the suppression branch, FREEZE the tracker — just `continue`, don't `reset()`. A pre-existing DOWN verdict is *real*, not stale; preserving it means a genuine child recovery still fires its DOWN→OK edge once the parent is back. One line removed. `reset()` remains, still called by `activate()` (a fresh arm should not inherit a stale verdict) — so the 16/16 `_CheckTracker` test is unaffected, and the fix simply makes `_poll_once` rely on the already-tested `down → OK → 'recovered'` path (test 5). `py_compile` + 16/16 re-confirmed.
  - **Files:** `src/homelab_monitor.py` (NEW — `_CheckTracker`, `Check`, `HomelabMonitor`, `HOMELAB_STATUS_TOOL` + `execute_homelab_status`, the tunables; the live-test freeze fix in `_poll_once`); `src/notifications.py` (`send_discord_message` — generic plain-text webhook push); `src/plex_laptop.py` (`fetch_disk_usage` — public structured-data seam over `_HEALTH_DISK_PS`); `src/config.py` (`homelab_monitor_enabled`); `src/llm.py` (`homelab_status` registered + system-prompt entry #22); `main.py` (construct always, `activate()` on the flag, tray wiring, `shutdown()`); `src/ui.py` + `src/tray.py` (the "Homelab monitoring" tray toggle, the Security-mode pattern); `.env.example` (`JARVIS_HOMELAB_*`); `scripts/homelab_alert_test.py` (NEW); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M56):** (1) **A monitoring tool's hard part is alert hygiene, not the probe.** Connecting a socket is trivial; *not* crying wolf is the engineering. Flap damping + edge-triggering + dependency suppression are the difference between a tool you keep on and one you mute in a week — and they're the textbook ideas (Nagios soft/hard state, parent/child dependencies, the Schmitt trigger), worth naming. (2) **Isolate the decision from the I/O so the decision is testable.** `_CheckTracker` has no sockets, no threads — just `observe(ok) -> edge`. That let a 16-case asserting harness pin down the exact-once-per-edge behaviour that's otherwise only observable by taking Plex down in production. (3) **Add a seam, don't parse a display string.** `fetch_disk_usage` reuses the same PowerShell script as the voice-formatted health tool but returns structured data — a program acting on numbers should get numbers, not regex prose meant for a human ear. (4) **The powerful feature was a recombination.** M56's reach is the M24 SSH client, its push is the M38 webhook, its thread is the M53 scheduler shape, its lifecycle is the M34 watcher — the new code is mostly the alert state machine. A well-factored codebase makes the big new capability small.

- [x] **Milestone 57 — the "Arc Reactor" UI redesign (desktop console + iPhone PWA)**:
  - **What ships:** a futuristic Iron-Man / J.A.R.V.I.S. visual redesign of *both* of Jarvis's faces — the desktop customtkinter console and the iPhone PWA remote console. 56 milestones of capability had outgrown an M17-era UI; this is the face catching up to the brain. Direction chosen from three ASCII-mocked options presented side by side (Arc Reactor / HUD Terminal / Clean Stark) — **Arc Reactor**: one strong glowing centrepiece.
  - **Desktop console (`src/console.py` — commit 8e3abe0).** A visual-layer reskin ONLY: every public method and the transcript / waveform / status / thumbnail plumbing is byte-untouched. The 22px flat state dot becomes a layered **arc-reactor orb** drawn on the existing `tk.Canvas` — a bezel + 12 tick marks, two counter-rotating arcs (`create_arc` + an animated `start` angle), a 4-disc radial glow halo (filled ovals shaded dim→bright = a *faked* radial gradient — Tk has no real blur), and a breathing core. Colour tracks state; while SPEAKING the core throbs with the live TTS amplitude (`_wave_amplitude`) — the reactor reacting to Jarvis's own voice. Animated at 30 fps by `_tick_orb`, its own recursive `.after()` chain (the `_wave_tick` pattern). Palette: near-black `#0a0e14` + electric arc-reactor cyan `#22d3ee`, Iron-Man gold for THINKING — a console-local `_STATE_COLOR` map, deliberately NOT `tray.py`'s `State.value` (which still drives the tray icon). Dark native title bar via a `DwmSetWindowAttribute` ctypes call (`DWMWA_CAPTION_COLOR` 35 + immersive-dark 20) — fixes the Windows accent colour bleeding gold through the caption. HUD corner brackets + thin glowing borders on the transcript/input; the waveform gains a faint idle shimmer + hot-peak colouring. Bigger fonts throughout (header 22→28, state 14→18, transcript 11→13, …).
  - **iPhone PWA (`src/remote_pwa.py`).** The same Arc Reactor look, web-native: matching palette, and a **CSS arc-reactor orb** in the header — concentric rings, a rotating `::before` arc, a breathing `box-shadow` glow. The web gets a *real* glow (unlike Tk's faked one), so the PWA orb genuinely shines. State-reactive via a `.s-*` class. HUD restyle: letter-spaced glowing wordmark, cyan-glow panel borders, retinted bubbles, cyan Send button. **Deliberately CSS-heavy + only ~10 lines of isolated, bulletproof JS** — a `setOrb()` that sets a CSS class from a 3-item whitelist (cannot throw), called from the existing `snapshot` / `state` message handlers. The WebSocket / reconnect-watchdog / push-to-talk / audio / token machinery is byte-identical.
  - **Risk-proportionate rigour.** The desktop console is not the demo surface, so it was committed after a live preview across all four states. The PWA *is* the surface others see, so: CSS-mostly to minimise logic risk; the institutionalised `scripts/js_parse_gate.py` re-run (PASS — balanced, every function exactly 1×, the M48.3 machinery intact); committed ONLY after verification on the real iPhone — look *and* a functional pass (message round-trip, mic, arm/disarm); isolated single-file commits so a revert is one command. Same redesign, two different rigour bars — proportionate to blast radius.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS (both files); `js_parse_gate.py` PASS; desktop live-previewed across idle / listening / thinking / speaking via a throwaway `scripts/_ui_preview.py` (built, used, deleted — the M48-spike pattern); PWA **live-validated on the real iPhone** — clean look, functional pass clean.
  - **Files:** `src/console.py` (the orb — `_build_orb` / `_draw_orb` / `_tick_orb`, colour helpers `_dim` / `_blend`, `_add_corner_brackets`, `_apply_dark_titlebar`, the `_STATE_COLOR` map; palette + every widget restyled); `src/remote_pwa.py` (palette, `.orb` CSS + `@keyframes`, the header orb element, `setOrb` + 2 call sites, `STATE_COLOR` re-shaded, manifest/theme colours); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M57):** (1) **A redesign should be a visual-layer swap, not a re-architecture.** Both files kept 100% of their functional plumbing — the discipline was confining the change to colours / fonts / canvas-drawing / CSS and a tiny, provably-safe wiring delta. That is what made a sweeping look-change low-risk. (2) **Show, don't tell, for a design decision.** Three ASCII-mockup options presented together made the choice a single glance instead of a paragraph of description and a guess. (3) **The platform decides how good an effect can be.** Tk has no blur, so the orb glow is faked with concentric filled ovals; the web has real `box-shadow`, so the PWA orb genuinely glows. Same design, two honest implementations — don't fight the platform, exploit it. (4) **Match the risk gate to the surface.** The desktop shipped after a preview; the PWA earned the extra `js_parse_gate` + a mandatory real-device functional pass before commit. Rigor should scale with blast radius, not be uniform.

- [x] **Milestone 58 — acoustic awareness (the audio twin of M34 vision security)**:
  - **What ships:** Jarvis hears the non-speech world. A background classifier listens on its own mic stream and speaks up unprompted on a doorbell, knock, smoke / fire alarm, glass breaking, phone ringing, kitchen timer, or a sink left running. The reactive→proactive jump for the AUDIO channel — he sees (M34/M30) and now he hears. Same dual-channel as homelab alerts: spoken via the WASAPI-safe Announcer (tagged 🔔) + Discord push to the phone.
  - **The risk that mattered most — the model-sourcing de-risk.** Initial pick was AST (Audio Spectrogram Transformer) via the existing `transformers` pipeline — strongest AudioSet model, zero new deps, looked perfect on paper. **Measured early: ~3.5 s steady-state CPU inference**, far too slow for a 1 Hz alert loop (the queue would back up forever and a doorbell alert would arrive 7 s+ late). Pivoted to PANNs Cnn14 via `panns_inference` (~325 MB checkpoint from Zenodo, one-time download). **Measured again: ~0.20 s steady-state** — 17× faster, comfortably real-time. The lesson re-cashed: *measure latency before you ship, not in production*.
  - **A bundled-downloader landmine.** `panns_inference` shells out to `wget` for its labels CSV and the checkpoint — broken on Windows (no `wget`). Wrote `_ensure_files()` to pre-place both via the project's existing `httpx`, atomic-write (temp + `os.replace`) so a partial download can't poison the next run. The import-time `from panns_inference import …` now Just Works.
  - **Architecture.** New module `src/sound_detector.py` with a `SoundDetector` class (modeled on `SecurityWatcher`/`HomelabMonitor`). Owns its **own** `sounddevice.InputStream` at 32 kHz mono (PANNs' training rate) — the existing `AudioSession` is single-reader-by-design (the wake-word loop owns it, the M52 barge-in monitor takes over during TTS), so adding a third concurrent reader would race; M58 captures independently, supported on Windows/WASAPI. Audio callback accumulates 2 s windows; inference worker pulls from a bounded queue and applies per-class alert hygiene (the M56 `_CheckTracker` discipline, generalised to add a wall-clock cooldown). 7 classes, 14 AudioSet label aliases — **all aliases matched the model's 527-class label set, zero missing**, verified during the de-risk.
  - **The continuous-water-source mitigation — a volume guard.** Surfaced at design time: the deployment environment contains a continuous low-level household water source running at room-ambient level. Without a guard, the `running_water` class would fire 24/7. So that one class is gated on **both** (a) classifier confidence and (b) audio RMS above a calibrated floor (`JARVIS_ACOUSTIC_WATER_RMS_FLOOR`, default 0.025) — a continuous low-level trickle sits well below the floor; a sink left on full sits comfortably above it. The other six classes are sharp transients naturally above ambient, no guard needed. Plus a **per-class kill switch** (`JARVIS_ACOUSTIC_DISABLE=running_water,...`) so any single class can be muted in `.env` without disabling the whole feature — the right escape hatch for an experimental class.
  - **Live-test PASS — 2026-05-26.** First arm logged `model loaded in 5.7s — 7/7 rules resolved` (model cached from the de-risk download). Three test events were triggered; ALL three fired correctly, ZERO false positives:
    - `FIRE doorbell (score=0.80 rms=0.049)` → *"Sir — that sounded like the doorbell."* + Discord HTTP 204
    - `FIRE phone_ringing (score=0.27 rms=0.016)` → *"Sir — your phone is ringing."* + Discord HTTP 204
    - `FIRE knock (score=0.29 rms=0.107)` → *"Sir — there's someone at the door."* + Discord HTTP 204
    A single `[audio] status: input overflow` at the moment the second `InputStream` started concurrent with the wake-word session — a one-shot cosmetic bootstrap blip, no clustering (the speech-gate starvation signature is *recurring* overflows over time, not a single startup line) and no missed events.
  - **Same-session enhancement — couple acoustic awareness to security mode.** The design shipped the two as independent toggles (the argument: acoustic has value while the machine is attended too — a doorbell you'd otherwise miss, a smoke alarm anytime). Real use flipped that within an hour: the operating mental model is `armed = unattended = listen to everything`, so acoustic sensing should follow the arm state. Wired in one chained callback: `security_watcher.set_on_armed_changed(...)` now both updates the UI indicator AND `sound_detector.activate()`/`.deactivate()`. The coupling is one-way (security drives acoustic, never the reverse); the independent tray toggle still works for the attended case; `activate()`/`deactivate()` are idempotent so a manual flip followed by a coupled flip never double-fires. Voice "activate security" and the tray toggle both now flip BOTH.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS (all `src/*.py`); `_ClassTracker` unit test **12/12 PASS** (`scripts/acoustic_alert_test.py`); import smoke OK with all 7 rules; **PANNs steady-state ~0.20 s/inference measured directly** in the de-risk harness. End-to-end live-validated 2026-05-26: 3 distinct events fired correctly, 0 false positives, the security-coupling lands cleanly.
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** the seven confirmed classes only. Not built: more classes (alarm clock, dog barking — listed as optional in the design Q+A but scoped out of v1); per-class threshold env override (currently only the volume floor is tunable); a "Jarvis, what did you just hear?" tool (would need to expose recent classifier scores to the LLM — a clean v2). And the volume floor (`0.025`) is a guessed-conservative starting value, not a calibrated one — the M44.1 lesson — expect to tune it against the actual continuous water source after a few real days.
  - **Files:** `src/sound_detector.py` (NEW — `SoundDetector` + `_ClassTracker` + `ClassRule` + `_DEFAULT_RULES` + `_ensure_files()` + the inference loop; the volume guard for running_water; PANNs Cnn14 via `panns_inference`); `requirements.txt` (`panns_inference>=0.1`); `src/config.py` (`acoustic_monitor_enabled`); `main.py` (construct always, `activate()` on the flag, tray wiring, shutdown, **the security-acoustic coupling**); `src/security.py` (`set_on_armed_changed` — small public setter for the coupling); `src/ui.py` + `src/tray.py` ("Acoustic awareness" tray toggle, the M56 pattern); `.env.example` (`JARVIS_ACOUSTIC_*`); `scripts/acoustic_alert_test.py` (NEW); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M58):** (1) **Measure latency before you ship, not in production.** AST looked perfect on paper but was 17× too slow on this CPU. A 10-line smoke test (model + 6 inferences) saved the milestone from a broken-on-launch outcome. (2) **A bundled downloader is a Windows landmine.** `wget` is a Linux-ism; the `httpx` already in the project replaces it cleanly with atomic writes. Always check first-run-on-Windows for any package that fetches its own assets. (3) **Real use beats design-time reasoning.** The design argued for independent toggles; the first day of real use flipped it within an hour. Designs are hypotheses, real use is the experiment — keep coupling/decoupling easy so the UX can be adjusted in one line. (4) **A known environmental constraint is gold.** The continuous-water-source constraint was known before a line of code was written; the volume-guard mitigation exists *only* because of it. Always characterise the room before you trust a classifier.

- [x] **Milestone 59 — scheduled briefing (the M54 × M55 hook, finally cashed)**:
  - **What ships:** *"Brief me every weekday at 7"* now works. The M54 recurring-reminder scheduler can fire the M55 `get_briefing` composition at its slot instead of speaking a static message string. A single new field — `action="briefing"` on a reminder — bridges the two existing subsystems. `set_reminder(message="morning briefing", repeat={kind:"weekly", weekdays:[0,1,2,3,4], time:"07:00"}, action="briefing")` is the canonical call.
  - **Architecture — a one-field extension, not a new subsystem.** `add(message, fire_at, repeat, action=None)` gains the optional field; storage is forward-compatible (existing reminders without `action` behave unchanged). The scheduler's poll loop sniffs `rec.get("action")`: `None` → the existing synchronous `_fire_text` + `announce` path (cheap, a string); `"briefing"` → a new `_fire_briefing()` that **dispatches the composition on a throwaway worker thread**. The composition takes 5–10 s (fetches weather + news + reads the security event dir + the reminders store), so running it on the scheduler thread would block the poll loop for that whole window — the worker pattern keeps reminders snappy and the briefing slow-but-out-of-band.
  - **Time-of-day greeting.** A briefing scheduled for 6 pm shouldn't be greeted with *"Good morning, sir."* Added `_greeting_for(hour)` — morning before noon, afternoon up to 17:00, evening after. Tiny, but the alternative would be a tone-deaf evening "good morning."
  - **Briefings are reminders.** Listed by `list_reminders` with a `"(briefing)"` tag, cancelled by `cancel_reminder` exactly like any other. Many can coexist — *"brief me weekdays at 7 AND every evening at 6"* — they're all just reminders with the action set.
  - **System prompt teaches the new field.** `set_reminder`'s entry now says: *"if asked to be briefed on a schedule, call set_reminder with `action='briefing'` AND a `repeat` spec — the reminder triggers the get_briefing composition at fire time."*
  - **Unknown actions are rejected early.** A typo (`action="brefing"`) gets a voice-friendly *"I don't know the 'brefing' action, sir — only 'briefing' is supported right now"* rather than a silent never-fires-as-expected outcome. Forward-compatible at the storage layer (unknown values stored verbatim and treated as no-op by the scheduler) but strict at the tool boundary (the LLM's input is validated).
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; new regression test `scripts/scheduled_briefing_test.py` PASS — covers schema field present, `add(action=...)` storage, `_greeting_for` returns morning/afternoon/evening correctly, unknown action rejected at the tool boundary.
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** only one action (`briefing`). Other actions (a "good night wrap" — the symmetric end-of-day counterpart from the M58+ wish list, or fire arbitrary tools) are easy follow-ons but the design says ship the one we promised first. The action enum + the dispatch switch make adding a second action a few-line change.
  - **Files:** `src/reminders.py` (`add(action=...)`, `SET_REMINDER_TOOL` schema, `execute_set_reminder` validation + path, `_fire_briefing` + `_greeting_for` helpers, scheduler dispatch, `execute_list_reminders` tag); `src/llm.py` (system-prompt addendum on `set_reminder`); `scripts/scheduled_briefing_test.py` (NEW regression); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M59):** (1) **Two existing subsystems + one bridging field = a feature.** The M54 scheduler and the M55 composition were both already there; M59 is a single new field on the existing record and a single conditional dispatch in the existing loop. The smallest milestones are the ones where you find the right *bridge*, not where you build new infrastructure. (2) **Slow side-work belongs on a worker thread.** The scheduler's job is to be on-time, not to *do* the work. When the work is slow (5–10 s composition), dispatch it; keep the poller free for the next slot. (3) **Reject unknown values at the tool boundary, store them forward-compatibly underneath.** Strict at the LLM-facing edge keeps "did the model typo it?" obvious; lenient at the storage layer keeps a v2 from needing a migration. The two postures are not the same.

- [x] **Milestone 60 — self-status report ("Jarvis, are you healthy?")**:
  - **What ships:** `status_report` — Jarvis's own subsystem roll-call. *"Jarvis, status report"* → one line per subsystem (security, acoustic awareness, homelab monitor, Plex MCP, Plex laptop SSH, remote console, STT backend, reminders, process memory) plus a count of concerning lines in this session's log. The broader companion to M56's `homelab_status` (which is the homelab-only view).
  - **Why this matters:** subsystems degrade *silently* by design (the project-wide fail-soft contract). The GPU STT server might be down, Plex MCP might never have connected, the remote console might be off — and Jarvis just keeps running. That's correct behaviour, but it means a quiet failure can persist for days. `status_report` makes the quiet state legible on demand.
  - **Architecture — a registry, not a god object.** New module `src/self_status.py` with `register(name, getter) → ONE status line`. Each subsystem registers a closure in `main.py` at startup (after every dependency is constructed, so each getter captures the live local). The tool iterates the registry in insertion order, joins the lines. Same module-singleton pattern M56 uses for the live monitor — no subsystem refs threaded through `stream_response`; the tool simply reads what main.py has registered. **Order is display order**: security first (the most-checked state), then the M58/M56/M48/STT subsystems, then telemetry (reminders / memory / errors).
  - **Concerning lines in `jarvis.log`.** `count_session_errors()` scans from the most recent `--- Jarvis started …` session marker forward, counting matches of `\b(failed|raised|tripped|traceback|panic|exception)\b` — patterns observed in real Jarvis logs (`[homelab] poll failed`, `[security] MEMORY WATCHDOG TRIPPED`, `[acoustic] inference call failed`, plus Python's `Traceback:` / `Exception:`). The word "error" itself is deliberately excluded — too noisy (`"no error"`, `permission_error` in arg names). Returns `(count, context)` so the report line can say "since session start" vs. "in recent log tail" (the fallback when no session marker exists, e.g. after log rotation). Bounded by jarvis.log's 5 MB rotation cap — the whole-file read is cheap.
  - **Defensive end-to-end:** an individual getter that raises is logged and renders as `"name: error reading (RuntimeError)"`; the rest of the report continues. Never one bad subsystem breaks the whole roll-call.
  - **Prompt teaches concision.** The `status_report` entry tells Claude to read the result back *concisely* — *"if everything is healthy, say so in one sentence ('all systems nominal, sir'); only enumerate the problem subsystems unless a full report was explicitly asked for."* Same fresh-state discipline as the briefing and homelab_status — re-run every time.
  - **NOT in `_RESTRICTED_DENY`.** The phone may ask "are you healthy?" — it's read-only, exposes no secrets, touches nothing. Consistent with `plex_laptop_health` and `homelab_status`.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; new regression test `scripts/status_report_test.py` PASS — **7/7**: empty registry returns a clear message; getters render in insertion order; a raising getter renders as `error reading` and the report continues; `count_session_errors` scans correctly across the *most recent* session marker (older session's errors are excluded — proven with two consecutive markers and verified the count is 3, not 6); fallback to the recent-tail when no marker; missing log file is fail-soft `(0, 'no log file yet')`; tool schema is well-formed. Import smoke confirms `status_report` is in `_CLIENT_TOOLS` and correctly absent from `_RESTRICTED_DENY`.
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** the 10 subsystems registered are the ones Jarvis has *as of M60*. Future subsystems should register themselves alongside their construction — one `register("My subsystem", lambda: …)` line in main.py is the contract. NOT built: a "Discord push the status on schedule" thing (a scheduled briefing already covers that role); per-subsystem severity (green/yellow/red); a `last_event_age` field for each subsystem (e.g. "homelab last alert: 14 minutes ago") — clean v2 additions on top of the registry.
  - **Files:** `src/self_status.py` (NEW — `_REGISTRY`, `register()`, `count_session_errors()`, `process_private_mb()`, `STATUS_REPORT_TOOL`, `execute_status_report()`); `src/llm.py` (import + `_CLIENT_TOOLS` + tools list + system-prompt entry #23); `main.py` (the registration block — 10 getters registered after every subsystem is constructed); `scripts/status_report_test.py` (NEW 7/7); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M60):** (1) **A registry beats a god object.** Instead of one big `status_report` function that hardcodes every subsystem it knows about, each subsystem registers its own getter. Adding a new subsystem is one line at its construction site; no central file to "remember" to update. The Open/Closed principle in 30 lines of Python. (2) **Be specific about your error patterns.** `\b(failed|raised|tripped|traceback)\b` catches real Jarvis log lines; matching the bare word `"error"` would have buried the signal in routine `permission_error`-style false positives. *Know the patterns of the system you're scanning.* (3) **Fresh-state requests deserve a re-run, every time.** The briefing (M55), homelab status (M56), and now status_report all share this — the prompt entry says "re-run every time, never answer from an earlier call." Three milestones, one pattern; once you have it, nail it everywhere.

- [x] **Milestone 61 — `knowledge_remember` (the LLM-facing knowledge-base WRITE tool)**:
  - **What ships & why:** a natural spoken request to durably remember a personal fact ("I want you to remember the names of my pets") got the reply *"I don't have a write tool for the knowledge base, sir."* That gap is closed. `knowledge_remember` is the LLM-facing companion to M45's `knowledge_search` — Claude can now write a durable fact to the corpus on any natural "remember this for me" phrasing.
  - **Why the gap existed.** The M45 voice-intent path (`_REMEMBER_INTENT_RE`) deliberately requires a permanence adverb — *"remember this **permanently**: …"* / *"forever"* / *"for good"* — to disambiguate from episodic *"remember when we…"* (a different store). A natural phrasing like *"I want to remember"* has no adverb, so the regex didn't fire, and Claude had no Anthropic tool to fall back on. The two paths are intentional: the regex captures the unambiguous "do this NOW without involving the LLM" case; the tool covers everything else.
  - **Implementation — a one-tool add.** Schema (`KNOWLEDGE_REMEMBER_TOOL`) + executor (`execute_knowledge_remember`) live in `src/knowledge.py` next to their search siblings; the executor wraps the existing `remember_fact()` so behaviour is byte-identical to the voice-intent path (one dated `.md` per fact, atomic write, reindex on success, voice-ready confirmation, never raises). Wired in `src/llm.py`: import, `_CLIENT_TOOLS`, the `tools` list, and the system-prompt knowledge section is split into 14 (read) and 14b (write) with clear "use this when" / "don't use this for" guidance — episodic memory and `set_reminder` are explicitly named as different stores to keep the LLM's mental model crisp.
  - **NOT in `_RESTRICTED_DENY`** — consistent with `set_reminder` (the other mutating-user-data tool the phone is allowed to use). The corpus is its own git repo pushed offsite, so any spam from a compromised phone is fully reversible. The least-privilege ladder can be tightened later if it ever bites.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; new regression test `scripts/knowledge_remember_test.py` **9/9 PASS** — schema name + description + required field; empty-fact returns voice-friendly error AND writes no file; real fact writes exactly one `.md` file containing the fact text (verified by reading the file body back — both distinctive tokens of the fact present); reindex ran (the M46 semantic-search pipeline loaded all-MiniLM-L6-v2 + indexed 1 chunk); the tool is registered in `_CLIENT_TOOLS` and correctly absent from `_RESTRICTED_DENY`. Uses `JARVIS_KNOWLEDGE_DIR` + a `tempfile.TemporaryDirectory()` so the real corpus isn't littered.

  **Reusable lessons (M61):** (1) **Intent-regex vs. LLM tool: pick BOTH, not either.** A strict regex captures "definitely do X" deterministically; a tool covers every conversational variation the regex won't. They're complementary, not redundant — the regex bypasses the LLM (faster, certain), the tool gives the LLM the same verb when the phrasing is more natural. Most "remember this" features should ship both, with the regex requiring an unambiguous keyword and the tool taking everything else. (2) **A gap surfaced by real use is data, not noise.** The exact failing transcript becomes the regression test case. Capture it, run it through the new path, ship.

- [x] **Milestone 62 — Outlook calendar awareness (read-only, Microsoft Graph)**:
  - **What ships:** the morning briefing gains *today's calendar events*, and Claude can answer *"what's on my calendar?"*, *"do I have anything later?"*, *"what's tomorrow looking like?"*, *"am I free at 3pm?"* via a new `get_calendar_events` tool. Personal Microsoft accounts (outlook.com / hotmail.com / live.com) in v1; work/school accounts are mechanically the same but need different Azure tenant config + potentially IT admin consent, deferred. Read-only by hard contract — `Calendars.Read` scope, no create/modify capability.
  - **Architecture — MSAL + Graph, two paths separated by design.** The interactive OAuth and the runtime queries are deliberately on different code paths:
    - **One-shot interactive setup:** `scripts/outlook_auth.py` runs the MSAL device-code flow. Prints a code + URL, entered at microsoft.com/devicelogin; refresh token saved to `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Jarvis\msal_cache.bin`. Runs ONCE.
    - **Silent runtime:** every tool / briefing call uses `acquire_token_silent()` — fast, no user interaction, MSAL auto-refreshes before expiry. If the cache is missing or revoked, the tool returns a clear *"run `python scripts/outlook_auth.py` to re-authorise"* message rather than blocking on an interactive prompt. **A voice assistant cannot usefully run device-code in the middle of a tool call** — it would block Claude's reply for up to 15 minutes. Separating the paths is the key UX call.
  - **Graph endpoint: `/me/calendarView`**, NOT `/me/events`. `/calendarView` expands recurring events into instances — so "today's events" correctly includes the Nth occurrence of a weekly recurring meeting, which `/events` would miss (it returns only series masters + exceptions). Times come back in UTC by default (no `Prefer: outlook.timezone="…"` header — avoids the Windows-vs-IANA timezone-name compatibility headache); we convert to local on display so the wall clock reads correctly.
  - **Briefing integration.** New `_calendar_section()` in `src/briefing.py` slots between security and reminders — the logical position for "the day's schedule" in the morning briefing. **Three states, each handled distinctly:** (a) `OUTLOOK_CLIENT_ID` unset ⇒ section silently omitted (no nag when calendar was never configured); (b) configured but auth expired / Graph erroring ⇒ section SURFACES the error verbatim (silent failure here would mean a missed meeting); (c) configured + working but no events ⇒ "Calendar: nothing scheduled today." (positive empty-state).
  - **Honest about the setup required.** Unlike most milestones this one needs a real ~10-minute Azure app registration before it works. The full step-by-step lives in `.env.example` — portal.azure.com → AAD → App registrations → New (Personal Microsoft accounts only) → enable public client flows → add Calendars.Read delegated permission → copy Application ID into `.env`. Then `python scripts/outlook_auth.py` for the one-time OAuth. After that, transparent.
  - **NOT in `_RESTRICTED_DENY`.** Read-only, exposes only the account's own calendar (already on the phone via the Outlook app). Phone may ask "what's on my calendar?" — consistent with how `homelab_status` and `status_report` are phone-allowed.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; `scripts/outlook_calendar_test.py` **20/20 PASS** — schema well-formed; the unconfigured path produces a voice-friendly setup message; invalid timeframe enums rejected with the valid options named; `_window_for` resolves all 4 timeframes correctly (`today`/`tomorrow`/`this_week`/`next_24h`); `_parse_graph_dt` handles Graph's ISO 8601 quirks (naive UTC, trailing 'Z', 7-digit fractional seconds — the `.0000000` Python's `fromisoformat` chokes on); `_normalise` gracefully handles missing optional fields + malformed dates (returns None, doesn't raise); `_format_events` covers empty / one / multi-event / with-location / all-day cases. **Live end-to-end pending the Azure registration + first OAuth** — the test deliberately doesn't make a live Graph call (would need a token + a test tenant).
  - **Deliberate v1 scope:** read tool + briefing integration. NOT built: **pre-event proactive reminders** (the wish-list item's second half — *"Jarvis, you have a meeting in 15 minutes"*) — a background poller that scans the next 30 minutes and announces N-minutes-out — a clean fast-follow (M62.1) on top of this read layer. NOT built: work-account / multi-account support; DPAPI encryption of the token cache (currently NTFS ACL alone — adequate for single-user desktop use; `msal-extensions` is the upgrade); writing events (a different scope, a different tool).
  - **Files:** `src/outlook_calendar.py` (NEW — `_OAuthClient` MSAL wrapper, `_fetch_events`, `_normalise`, `_parse_graph_dt`, `today_events`, `CalendarEvent` dataclass, `GET_CALENDAR_TOOL` + `execute_calendar_tool`); `scripts/outlook_auth.py` (NEW — the one-shot device-code setup); `scripts/outlook_calendar_test.py` (NEW 20/20); `src/llm.py` (import + `_CLIENT_TOOLS` + tools list + system-prompt entry #24, reordered so 22→23→24 read in numeric order); `src/briefing.py` (`_calendar_section()` + reordered the sections list + filter empty sections); `requirements.txt` (`msal>=1.30`); `.env.example` (full Azure walkthrough); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M62):** (1) **Separate interactive auth from runtime queries.** Any feature that needs OAuth has TWO paths: a one-shot interactive setup (blocks for a human) and a silent runtime (never blocks). Conflating them — calling `device-code-flow` from inside a chat tool — would block Claude's reply for minutes. Two scripts, two responsibilities. (2) **Pick `/calendarView` over `/events` whenever recurrence matters.** Graph's `/events` returns series masters; `/calendarView` expands them. For "today's events" you want the expansion, every time. Read the docs for the endpoint you're calling; defaults aren't always what you assume. (3) **Surface auth errors; silently skip unconfigured features.** An install that never set up calendar should NOT see a "calendar not configured" line in the briefing every morning (nag fatigue); an expired token SHOULD surface (a missed meeting is worse than a status line). Three states, three handlings — explicit. (4) **A milestone that requires setup needs the setup doc IN the repo.** The Azure click-through is real work; baking the steps into `.env.example` (where the env var is already being looked up) removes the context switch.

  - **M62.1 — published iCal URL backend (the path that actually shipped, same-day pivot).** Microsoft refused to cooperate: the portal.azure.com sign-in required for the M62 app registration failed with `AADSTS5000225: This tenant has been blocked due to inactivity` — Microsoft's [Tenant Lifecycle policy](https://aka.ms/TenantLifecycle) auto-suspending the implicit personal-account-backed Entra tenant. With no way past Microsoft's portal there's no way to register a Graph app, so the M62 Graph path was dead-on-arrival for this account. The iCal URL path (option C from the original M62 design Q&A) is the natural pivot — Outlook.com's "Publish a calendar" feature emits a secret `.ics` URL; Jarvis fetches it with `httpx` and expands recurring events client-side with `recurring-ical-events` (the analog of Graph's `/calendarView`). NO Azure registration, NO OAuth, NO Microsoft tenant required. Read-only by construction (publish is read-only). Setup: ~2 minutes in Outlook.com settings vs. ~10 minutes in Azure Portal.
  - **Architecture — two backends, one tool surface.** Added `_fetch_events_ical()` (httpx → `icalendar.Calendar.from_ical()` → `recurring_ical_events.of(cal).between(start, end)` → `_normalise_ical_event()` → list[CalendarEvent]) alongside the existing `_fetch_events_graph()` (renamed from `_fetch_events`). A new top-level `_fetch_events()` is the dispatcher: iCal wins when both are set (simpler, fewer failure modes), Graph is the fallback, neither configured returns a clear "set one of OUTLOOK_ICAL_URL or OUTLOOK_CLIENT_ID" message. `today_events()`, `execute_calendar_tool()`, and the briefing's `_calendar_section()` are backend-agnostic — they call the dispatcher and get `CalendarEvent` instances either way. The M62 Graph code stays in the module untouched (future infrastructure if the tenant is ever unblocked, or for anyone else picking the project up).
  - **One bug caught in the live end-to-end:** `_calendar_section()` had a `if not CLIENT_ID` silent-omit check that pre-dated the iCal path — would have made the briefing silently skip the calendar section for ICAL-only configurations. Caught when the live test returned an empty section; fixed to `if not (CLIENT_ID or ICAL_URL)`. The kind of bug only an actual end-to-end run finds; py_compile + unit tests can't catch it because the check is correct in isolation but wrong against the *expanded* configuration surface.
  - **One security note worth capturing:** the published iCal URL is a long-token bearer credential — anyone with the URL can read the calendar. Less sensitive than an OAuth refresh token (read-only, easily revoked from Outlook publish settings with one click — "Stop publishing" yields 404), but still a secret. `.env` discipline applies; `.gitignore` already blocks `.env`. Documented in `.env.example` so this is conscious risk, not accidental.
  - **One caveat worth knowing:** Microsoft caches the published feed server-side, so updates can lag the live calendar by up to a few hours. Acceptable for a daily briefing; an event created five minutes ago might not show until Microsoft refreshes the cache. The cache TTL isn't documented; empirically a few hours.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; `scripts/outlook_calendar_test.py` **33/33 PASS** (20 from M62 + **13 new for M62.1**: ICS single-event parse + `_normalise_ical_event` field extraction + all-day flag + recurrence expansion of `FREQ=WEEKLY;BYDAY=MO` into 4 instances + dispatcher precedence across all 4 env combinations: `both/graph-only/ical-only/neither`). **Live end-to-end PASS** — fetched a real published `.ics` from `outlook.live.com` (8 KB, `text/calendar`, PRODID `Microsoft Exchange Server 2010`), parsed clean, dispatcher correctly picked the iCal path, `execute_calendar_tool` returned "Nothing on your calendar today, sir." (the queried window genuinely had no events — empty-state path verified), briefing section returned "Calendar: nothing scheduled today." after the bug fix.
  - **Files:** `src/outlook_calendar.py` (added `ICAL_URL` env, `_fetch_events_ical`, `_normalise_ical_event`, new dispatcher `_fetch_events`; renamed old `_fetch_events` → `_fetch_events_graph`; updated header docstring); `src/briefing.py` (the one-line bug fix on the config check); `scripts/outlook_calendar_test.py` (13 new tests); `requirements.txt` (`recurring-ical-events>=3.0`); `.env.example` (full publish-from-Outlook walkthrough + the security note about the URL being a bearer credential).

  **Reusable lessons (M62.1):** (1) **When an external dependency falls over, pivot — don't fight.** Microsoft's tenant lockout isn't fixable from our side in any reasonable timeframe; the iCal path was the design Q&A's option C and works *better* here anyway. The cost of a "we picked the wrong path" admission is small; the cost of a half-day arguing with vendor support is huge. (2) **Two backends behind one tool surface is cheap insurance.** The Graph code from M62 wasn't wasted — it's still in the repo for the day the tenant is unblocked. The dispatcher pattern + identical `CalendarEvent` output shape means a backend swap is invisible to every other layer (tool, briefing, system prompt, the tests already cover both). (3) **Live end-to-end finds config-shape bugs that unit tests can't.** The briefing's `if not CLIENT_ID` check passed every isolated unit test — it's locally correct. It only became wrong once the *configuration surface* expanded to include ICAL_URL. The only way to catch it is to actually run the full path against a configured environment. Always live-test the integration before claiming "shipped."

- [x] **Milestone 62.2 — pre-event proactive calendar reminders** *(2026-05-28)*:
  - **What ships:** Jarvis watches the same Outlook feed M62 reads on-demand and announces an event a fixed lead time before its start: *"Sir — your 2 pm meeting in 15 minutes."* The reactive→proactive jump for the CALENDAR channel — the same jump M56 made for the homelab, M58 for the audio world. The natural close on the M62 calendar arc; the M62 entry explicitly listed this as the v2 follow-on.
  - **Architecture — daemon poll thread + pure decision function + persisted dedupe.** `src/calendar_monitor.py` (NEW) follows the M56 shape exactly: a daemon thread polls every `_POLL_SECONDS` (default 60, min 30), wrapped so a single bad poll can never kill the thread. On each tick: fetch via the new `outlook_calendar.fetch_events_in_window(start_local, end_local)` (a tight `[now, now + lead + 5min]` window — anything starting later than that can't possibly be due in this poll, so no point pulling a full day), then run `_should_announce(ev, now, lead_min, announced)` per event. That decision function is **pure** (no I/O, no module globals, no `time.time()` dependency) — given an event, the current time, the lead window, and the set of already-announced IDs, return `(should_fire, mins_to_start)`. The whole interesting core isolated for testing.
  - **The firing rule** (in order, exact contract): (a) all-day → never fire (the morning briefing already surfaces these, and "in N minutes" is meaningless for them); (b) already started (secs ≤ 0) → never fire (no late nag — announcing a meeting we missed is just noise); (c) outside the lead window (rounded minutes > lead_min) → not yet (re-evaluate next poll); (d) already announced → suppress; (e) else → fire. Each rule is one test in `scripts/calendar_monitor_test.py`.
  - **Event ID = `(subject, start_local.isoformat())`.** Deliberate: a rescheduled meeting (same subject, new start) gets a NEW id and (correctly) fires a fresh announce — a moved meeting IS news. The alternative — using a stable server-side UID — would silently swallow reschedules.
  - **Dedupe persisted to disk** (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\Jarvis\calendar_announced.json`). Without persistence, a Jarvis restart between the 15-min announce and the meeting itself would re-announce. `DedupeStore` (also testable in isolation) handles load + mark + save with atomic temp+`os.replace` writes, pruned to entries < 24h (an old event ID can't recur — start_local is part of the ID — so retaining longer just bloats the file). Corrupt JSON / non-dict shape / bad timestamps all degrade to empty, never crash.
  - **Default ON when calendar is configured** — different from M56 (homelab, default OFF, opt-in) and M58 (acoustic, default OFF, opt-in). Configuring `OUTLOOK_ICAL_URL` or `OUTLOOK_CLIENT_ID` is itself the opt-in; the proactive layer IS the value-add. Kill switch is `JARVIS_CALENDAR_REMINDERS=0`. The mental-model split: M56/M58 are sense-the-world capabilities that can be intrusive (network calls, continuous audio capture), so opt-in is the right ergonomic; M62.2 is "act on what was already configured," so default-on is friendlier.
  - **Voice tagged 📅** (distinct from 🚨 security / 🖥 homelab / 🔔 acoustic / ⏰ M53 reminders). Discord push runs on a throwaway daemon so a slow POST never delays the next poll — same shape as M56's `_emit`.
  - **Self-status registration** — registered with the M60 registry as "Calendar reminders: active/off". The status_report tool description updated to include it in the named subsystems.
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; `scripts/calendar_monitor_test.py` **25/25 PASS** (3 event-id tests covering reschedule-as-new-id + 11 firing-rule tests covering every branch + edge cases like exact-lead-edge / 30 s-future / TBA-dated all-day + 11 DedupeStore tests covering fresh-load / mark+save / re-construct / snapshot-isolation / retain-hours-prune / corrupt-JSON / list-shaped-JSON / bad-ISO-timestamps). Tests deliberately don't fire a real fetch — that's the live path exercised every morning.
  - **Files:** `src/calendar_monitor.py` (NEW); `src/outlook_calendar.py` (added public `fetch_events_in_window`); `main.py` (construct + activate + self-status register + shutdown — mirrors the M56 block); `.env.example` (new section documenting `JARVIS_CALENDAR_REMINDERS` / `JARVIS_CALENDAR_LEAD_MIN` / `JARVIS_CALENDAR_POLL_SECONDS`); `scripts/calendar_monitor_test.py` (NEW); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M62.2):** (1) **A pure decision function is the cheap version of an alert state machine.** M56 needed `_CheckTracker` because alert hygiene (flap damping, dependency suppression) is itself the hard part. M62.2 needed nothing more than `_should_announce(ev, now, lead_min, announced) -> (bool, int)` — same testing leverage, far less surface. Match the abstraction to the problem; the M56 pattern is overkill for a simpler version. (2) **The event id is a design decision, not a detail.** `(subject, start_local)` makes a reschedule = new announce; `event.uid` would make it = silent. Both can be right; pick the one that matches the desired behaviour ("a moved meeting is news"). (3) **Default-on vs default-off depends on what was already opted into.** M56/M58 are intrusive (continuous network probes / audio capture) → opt-in. M62.2 acts on an already-configured upstream → default-on. The pattern: if configuring the *upstream* is itself opt-in, the proactive layer can default-on without surprise.

- [x] **Milestone 63 — the "good night" wrap (the M55 symmetric counterpart)** *(2026-05-28)*:
  - **What ships:** *"Jarvis, good night"* / *"wrap up the day"* → one composed spoken wrap-up: **security state** + **tomorrow's first event** + **tomorrow's reminders** + **tomorrow's weather**. Bookends the day — M55 sets it up in the morning, M63 closes it out at night. The wish list called this *"mirror of M55, small"* and that is exactly what shipped — `src/good_night.py` is a near-clone of `src/briefing.py`'s composition shape (one tool, per-section fail-soft, never raises).
  - **Forward-looking, not backward.** A morning briefing tells you what TODAY will be; an evening wrap tells you what TOMORROW will be. The sections are deliberately all forward — tomorrow's calendar / reminders / weather — plus current security state (which IS today's state and is what you actually want to hear last thing before sleep). A "today's recap" section would have needed a per-fire reminders log the M53 store doesn't keep; deliberately deferred.
  - **Security state via injected getter** (the homelab_monitor decoupling pattern). `register_security_getter(security_watcher.is_armed)` wired in main.py at startup; the module-level `_security_getter` singleton lets the tool report current armed state without `security_watcher` threaded through `stream_response`. Unset getter ⇒ section silently omits (graceful degradation if good_night is somehow loaded before main.py wires the callback). Raising getter ⇒ section degrades to "Security: status unavailable" — never the whole wrap.
  - **Tomorrow's weather via a new public `weather.tomorrow_forecast(location, units)` helper.** Additive to the stable weather module (one new public symbol; same minimal-touch pattern as M62.2's `fetch_events_in_window`). Reuses the existing 5-day fetch + pulls day index 1 — no duplicate format code, no parsing the title-mode's formatted text (brittle).
  - **Tomorrow's first event: TIMED events only.** All-day items show in the morning briefing; "first thing" is when you have to actually be somewhere. `[e for e in events if not e.is_all_day][0]` after the M62 dispatcher returns events for the tomorrow window.
  - **Tomorrow's reminders: filter `list_pending()` by `fire_at.date() == (now + 1d).date()`.** Mirrors `briefing.py:_reminders_section` shifted forward one day. Empty case is a positive empty-state ("Reminders tomorrow: none scheduled") not a silent omit.
  - **Scheduled wrap via the M59 hook** — `set_reminder action="good_night"` triggers `get_good_night` at fire time (e.g. every weeknight at 10 pm). One-field extension to the existing enum + one-line addition to a NEW dispatch dict. **The M59 generalisation is the architectural win.** `_fire_briefing` (singular, hardcoded to one composition) was refactored into a generic `_fire_composition` that reads `(composer, noun, listing_tag, failed_phrase)` from `_COMPOSITION_ACTIONS = {"briefing": (...), "good_night": (...)}`. Adding the next composition tool (e.g. a "weekly review") is now a one-line entry, not a copy-pasted fire function. The N=2 generalisation actually paid for itself in the same build.
  - **System prompt** gains item 22 (`get_good_night`), and items 23+ renumber. The M59 routing entry now covers both "brief me at 7am" AND "wrap me up at 10pm" paths verbatim. The tool description explicitly says re-run every time (fresh-state, like the morning briefing — never "I already wrapped up earlier").
  - **Validation:** `py_compile` PASS; `scripts/good_night_test.py` **27/27 PASS** (4 schema; 4 `_security_section` covering unset-getter / armed / standing-down / raising; 4 `_reminders_section` covering tomorrow-only filter / empty case; 1 `_calendar_section` silent-omit when unconfigured; 5 `execute_good_night_tool` end-to-end; 9 dispatch helpers `_action_label` + `_action_listing_tag` for both composition kinds). M59 regression test updated for the expanded enum + still PASS. **Live-validated** the same session.
  - **Files:** `src/good_night.py` (NEW); `src/weather.py` (added public `tomorrow_forecast`); `src/reminders.py` (extended enum from `["briefing"]` to `["briefing", "good_night"]`; refactored `_fire_briefing` → generic `_fire_composition` over `_COMPOSITION_ACTIONS`; new `_action_label` + `_action_listing_tag` helpers replacing inline `if action == "briefing"` strings); `src/llm.py` (import + `_CLIENT_TOOLS` + tools list + new system-prompt item 22 + renumber 22→23 / 23→24 / 24→25); `main.py` (register_security_getter wire-up); `scripts/good_night_test.py` (NEW); `scripts/scheduled_briefing_test.py` (updated for the expanded enum); `CLAUDE.md`; this entry.

  **Reusable lessons (M63):** (1) **N=2 is when "generalise vs duplicate" stops being a coin flip.** With one composition action (M59) a single `_fire_briefing` was right. With two, copy-pasting it would have been wrong — but only because the variants share a *named shape* (`(composer, noun, listing_tag, failed_phrase)`). If the second variant had needed bespoke logic that didn't fit the tuple, duplicating would still have been the right call. The dividing line is "can you name the tuple?", not "how many variants?". (2) **Injected getters scale better than threading dependencies.** `register_security_getter(security_watcher.is_armed)` keeps `good_night` independent of `security` — the tool finds live state through a module singleton, not a `stream_response` parameter. This is the same pattern as `homelab_monitor._set_active_monitor`; recognise it as a pattern, reuse it. (3) **Live behaviour is the final oracle on whether a gap is real.** The M48 phone state-pill "worked" by code-reading for a week (`{type:"state"}` fanned out, the PWA had the map) — but the actual device showed nothing, and that found the real bug (the value was an RGB tuple, not the state name). Code-reading tells you what should happen; the running system tells you what does.

- [x] **Milestone 64 — self-update ("Jarvis, update yourself")** *(2026-05-28)*:
  - **What ships:** `update_jarvis` tool — Claude pulls the latest code from origin and restarts Jarvis. *"Jarvis, update yourself"* / *"pull the latest"* / *"self-update"* / *"check for updates"*.
